The bilingual official logo of the election

Legislative elections were held in [Kazakhstan](16642.md) on 23 August 2026 to elect all 145 members of the [Kurultai](83119061.md) of the [1st convocation](83747507.md).[^cite_note-Reut-1][^cite_note-2] It was the 10th legislative election since Kazakhstan's [independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/On_the_State_Independence_of_the_Republic_of_Kazakhstan) in 1991 and the first election to the country's new [unicameral legislature](204304.md) established by the [2026 Constitution](83368720.md).[^cite_note-3][^cite_note-4] Turnout was 74.2%, making it the highest turnout recorded in a national vote in Kazakhstan since the [2026 referendum](82396297.md) and the highest turnout in a legislative election since [2016](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Kazakh_legislative_election).[^cite_note-5][^cite_note-6]

The new constitution, approved in a [constitutional referendum](82396297.md), provided for the dissolution of the previous bicameral [Parliament of Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_Kazakhstan) and established the Kurultai as the country's highest representative body.[^cite_note-7][^cite_note-8] It entered into force on 1 July 2026, on which day President [Kassym-Jomart Tokayev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kassym-Jomart_Tokayev) signed a decree calling the legislative election for 23 August.[^cite_note-Reut-1][^cite_note-9][^cite_note-10] The vote also marked the return of [party-list proportional representation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Party-list_proportional_representation) under the new electoral system for the first time since the [2021 legislative elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Kazakh_legislative_election).[^cite_note-Pelizzo-11]

The campaign was contested by seven political parties and focused primarily on socio-economic and governance issues.[^cite_note-12] Major campaign issues included living standards, taxation and the minimum wage; [inflation](38286.md) and the rising cost of essential goods; economic and industrial development, including regional growth and labour productivity; and the implementation of the new constitutional and parliamentary system.[^cite_note-13][^cite_note-14]

Ahead of the election, press freedom organizations reported increasing pressure on independent journalists, including arrests, criminal investigations, travel bans and restrictions on journalistic activity.[^cite_note-15] The [Organization for Security and Co-operation in Europe](22796.md) said the elections were "technically well prepared" but took place in an environment lacking political pluralism, with limited political alternatives and restrictions on [independent](2172378.md) candidates, civil society and media.[^cite_note-16][^cite_note-17]

The newly formed pro-presidential [Ädilet](83490988.md) party, the successor to [Amanat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amanat_\(political_party\)), won the election with 71.2% of the vote, followed by the [Auyl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auyl) with 6.3%, [Respublica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Respublica_\(Kazakh_political_party\)) with 5.6%, the [Aq Jol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aq_Jol) with 5.2%, and the [Nationwide Social Democratic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationwide_Social_Democratic_Party) (JSDP) with 5.1%.[^cite_note-18][^cite_note-19] The [People's Party of Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Party_of_Kazakhstan) (QHP) and [Baytaq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baytaq) failed to reach the 5% [electoral threshold](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_threshold),[^cite_note-20][^cite_note-21] with the QHP losing parliamentary representation for the first time since it entered the parliament in the [2012 legislative elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Kazakh_legislative_election).

On 28 August 2026, the newly elected Kurultai convened for its first session, where Tokayev nominated [Aibek Dädebai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aibek_D%C3%A4debai) as [chairman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chairman_of_the_Kurultai), and deputies subsequently [elected](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_Chairman_of_the_Kurultai_election) him unanimously.[^cite_note-22] The [government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bektenov_Government), led by Prime Minister [Oljas Bektenov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oljas_Bektenov), also resigned, with its members continuing to perform their duties until a new government was formed.[^cite_note-23] On 31 August, Tokayev nominated Bektenov for reappointment as prime minister, with the Kurultai approving the nomination and Tokayev subsequently appointing him to the position.[^cite_note-24][^cite_note-25]

## Background

Main articles: [2023 Kazakh legislative election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Kazakh_legislative_election) and [8th Parliament of Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/8th_Parliament_of_Kazakhstan)

The [8th Parliament of Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/8th_Parliament_of_Kazakhstan) convened following the [2023 legislative elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Kazakh_legislative_election) to the lower house [Mäjilis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C3%A4jilis), which were called after President [Kassym-Jomart Tokayev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kassym-Jomart_Tokayev) dissolved the [7th Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/7th_Parliament_of_Kazakhstan) as part of the final stage of the political reset of state institutions that followed the [2022 Kazakh unrest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Kazakh_unrest).[^cite_note-26][^cite_note-27] The elections were held under a reintroduced [mixed electoral system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mixed_electoral_system) approved in the [2022 constitutional referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Kazakh_constitutional_referendum), the first such system used since [2004](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_Kazakh_legislative_election).[^cite_note-28][^cite_note-29] [Amanat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amanat_\(political_party\)) (formerly Nur Otan) won a majority of 62 seats,[^cite_note-30] though reduced compared to previous convocations,[^cite_note-31] and the new composition of the Mäjilis included [independent](2172378.md) deputies elected from [single-member districts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constituencies_of_Kazakhstan),[^cite_note-32] representatives of the self-declared opposition [Nationwide Social Democratic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationwide_Social_Democratic_Party), and first-time parliamentary entrants [Auyl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auyl) and [Respublica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Respublica_\(Kazakh_political_party\)), bringing in a total of six party factions.[^cite_note-33]

From there, at the opening session of the [8th convocation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/8th_Parliament_of_Kazakhstan), [Erlan Qoşanov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erlan_Qo%C5%9Fanov) (Amanat) was re-elected as [chairman of the Mäjilis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chairman_of_the_M%C3%A4jilis), and the Parliament approved the reappointment of [Älihan Smaiylov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%84lihan_Smaiylov) as [prime minister of Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Kazakhstan).[^cite_note-34][^cite_note-35][^cite_note-36] During its tenure, the [8th convocation of the Parliament of Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/8th_Parliament_of_Kazakhstan) served as the final [bicameral legislature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bicameralism) before the transition to the unicameral [Kurultai](83119061.md), adopting legislation on social policy, economic regulation, digitalization, and institutional reforms while overseeing the final stage of Tokayev's post-2022 reform agenda, including constitutional law amendments implementing the transition to the new parliamentary system, before concluding its work on 30 June 2026.[^cite_note-37][^cite_note-38]

Discussions about a possible further constitutional amendments and snap parliamentary election emerged in 2024;[^cite_note-39][^cite_note-40] however, in January 2025, during an interview with the newspaper *Ana tili*, president Tokayev publicly denied plans for any snap elections, including parliamentary ones, stating that the 8th Parliament of Kazakhstan would serve its full term and that elections would be held in accordance with the law.[^cite_note-41][^cite_note-42][^cite_note-43]

Despite Tokayev ruling out early elections, ongoing discussions about future constitutional reforms, including proposals to abolish the [Senate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senate_of_Kazakhstan), speculation persisted about snap parliamentary elections with social activist Qazbek Beisebaev noting that any changes to parliament would first require [constitutional amendments](261586.md) or a [referendum](51582.md) before snap election, and while the current parliament is generally loyal to the government, other political actors might seek to renew the legislature for strategic purposes.[^cite_note-44] In June 2025, [chairman of the Senate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chairman_of_the_Senate_of_Kazakhstan) [Mäulen Äşimbaev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C3%A4ulen_%C3%84%C5%9Fimbaev) acknowledging rumours of early parliamentary elections allegedly taking place in autumn of that year, stated that there were no grounds to expect such elections.[^cite_note-45][^cite_note-46][^cite_note-47]

### 2026 constitutional reforms

Main article: [2026 Kazakh constitutional referendum](82396297.md)

In September 2025, during his State of the Nation Address, Tokayev announced proposed parliamentary reforms, including a constitutional amendment to abolish the [Senate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senate_of_Kazakhstan) and transform the [Parliament of Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_Kazakhstan) into a [unicameral legislature](204304.md) elected by [party-list](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_list), suggesting that the change be decided through a referendum sometime in 2027.[^cite_note-48] The announcement was followed by six-month span with the establishment of a [working group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Working_Group_on_Parliamentary_Reform) and later a constitutional commission tasked with drafting amendments.[^cite_note-49] During its work, the commission concluded that the scope of proposed amendments exceeded initial expectations and ultimately endorsed adopting a new constitution,[^cite_note-50][^cite_note-51] which its draft was finalized in February 2026 and subsequently submitted by Tokayev to a referendum.[^cite_note-52][^cite_note-53]

The referendum "Yes" vote campaign was organized under the National Coalition "For the People's Constitution of a Fair and Progressive Kazakhstan", bringing together pro-government political parties and public organizations in support of the draft constitution,[^cite_note-54] which proposed restructuring Kazakhstan's legislative system ahead of the transition to the newly unicameral [Kurultai](83119061.md).[^cite_note-55][^cite_note-56] The referendum campaign took place in a restrictive political environment marked by reported pressure on opponents and critics of the draft constitution, censorship, limits on independent polling, and rejected legal challenges,[^cite_note-57][^cite_note-58] with social media users referring to the draft constitution as "sour cream" to avoid crackdown.[^cite_note-59][^cite_note-60] The proposal was officially approved by 87% of voters, with Tokayev issuing a decree adopting the [new constitution](83368720.md) on 17 March 2026, which entered into force on 1 July 2026.[^cite_note-61][^cite_note-62]

Under the previous constitutional framework, the [8th convocation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/8th_Parliament_of_Kazakhstan) of the Mäjilis, elected in March 2023, would have served its five-year constitutional term. Under Article 51 of the 1995 Constitution, regular elections to the Mäjilis were required to be held within the two months preceding the expiration of deputies' term,[^cite_note-63] meaning that absent constitutional changes the next legislative elections would have been expected in early 2028.[^cite_note-64][^cite_note-65][^cite_note-66] However, the constitutional reforms initiated in 2025 fundamentally altered the structure and electoral timetable of the legislature. Although Tokayev initially proposed holding a referendum on parliamentary reform in 2027, the adoption of a new constitution in 2026 and the transition to a unicameral Kurultai required the dissolution of the existing bicameral system and the organization of early elections to form the new legislature.[^cite_note-67]

Following the approval of the new constitution, the [Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_Kazakhstan) adopted a series of seven constitutional laws to implement the new state structure.[^cite_note-68][^cite_note-69] The foundational laws on the presidency, Kurultai, and People's Council were signed by President [Kassym-Jomart Tokayev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kassym-Jomart_Tokayev) on 5 June 2026,[^cite_note-70][^cite_note-71][^cite_note-72] while amendments to existing constitutional laws, including changes affecting the electoral framework and political parties, were signed on 11 June 2026.[^cite_note-73]

### Minimum wage increase proposal

In the lead-up to the 2026 elections in Kazakhstan, economic concerns such as inflation, rising utility costs, and declining real incomes became a key issue in public debate, despite continued official references to macroeconomic growth. Against this backdrop, the Kazakh government's April 2026 proposal to raise the [national minimum wage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minimum_wage_in_Kazakhstan) from 85,000 to 150,000 tenge emerged as a prominent socio-economic pledge, with First Vice Minister of National Economy Azamat Amrin linking it to a target of bringing minimum wages closer to 50% of median income.[^cite_note-74] However, Minister of Labour and Social Protection [Asqarbek Ertaev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asqarbek_Ertaev) later clarified that the proposal remained under review, with no finalized methodology, budget decision, or implementation timeline, ruling out near-term adoption.[^cite_note-75]

Analysts interpreted the timing of the announcement amid preparations for the new unicameral Kurultai as carrying a clear pre-election dimension, with political scientist Daniyar Ashimbayev describing the proposal as having a "pre-election theme" and a populist character that signaled responsiveness to public concerns over living standards while functioning more as a politically salient promise than an immediate policy commitment, in a context where parliamentary elections are generally viewed as less politically destabilizing and where protest sentiment is often expected to be absorbed within pro-government parties.[^cite_note-76]

### Party system reorganization

The [Ädilet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%84dilet_\(political_party,_2026\)) party was announced in April 2026 by political activists, public figures, business representatives, and members of the Constitutional Commission amid Kazakhstan's post-referendum political restructuring following the adoption of a new constitution.[^cite_note-\:1-77] The party emerged ahead of the 2026 legislative elections as a centrist, pro-presidential political force supporting the state's reform agenda.[^cite_note-\:1-77]

Its rapid registration, prominent leadership, and close association with the constitutional reform process prompted extensive political commentary. Analysts characterized Ädilet as part of a broader reconfiguration of Kazakhstan's party system following the constitutional reforms, with some describing it as a move toward a more competitive [multi-party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multi-party_system) or quasi [two-party system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Two-party_system) and suggesting that it could reshape the distribution of pro-presidential and centrist voters ahead of the elections.[^cite_note-\:0-78][^cite_note-\:2-79] Political scientist Andrei Chebotarev described Ädilet as a potential "sparring partner" to ruling [Amanat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amanat_\(political_party\)) party, while other observers viewed the party as an attempt to project political renewal within the existing political framework.[^cite_note-\:2-79][^cite_note-\:3-80][^cite_note-81][^cite_note-82] The significance of Ädilet increased substantially following the 26th Amanat Extraordinary Congress on 12 June 2026, at which Amanat approved its merger into Ädilet.[^cite_note-83] The merger ended Amanat's 27-year existence as an independent political party and transformed Ädilet into the institutional successor to the former ruling party ahead of the legislative elections.[^cite_note-84]

The merger coincided with wider changes across Kazakhstan's party system ahead of the elections. During June 2026, several parliamentary parties underwent leadership changes.[^cite_note-85] [Dania Espaeva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dania_Espaeva) succeeded [Azat Peruaşev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azat_Perua%C5%9Fev) as chair of [Aq Jol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aq_Jol),[^cite_note-86][^cite_note-87] [Qairat Aituğanov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qairat_Aitu%C4%9Fanov) replaced [Serik Egizbaev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serik_Egizbaev) as chairman of [Auyl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auyl),[^cite_note-88][^cite_note-89] and [Nursultan Şoqanov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nursultan_%C5%9Eoqanov) was elected leader of the [People's Party of Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Party_of_Kazakhstan) following the resignation of [Ermukhamet Ertisbaev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ermukhamet_Ertisbaev).[^cite_note-90][^cite_note-91] Political analyst Nurbolat Nyshanbai argued that the simultaneous leadership transitions were unlikely to be coincidental, suggesting they reflected parties' adaptation to a new political period and a redefinition of their roles ahead of the Kurultai elections, while the absence of public conflicts during the changes reflected internal agreements and elite consensus within Kazakhstan's political system.[^cite_note-92] Other observers described the reshuffling as part of a broader renewal of political forces following the constitutional reforms and the transition to the new electoral framework.[^cite_note-93][^cite_note-94]

### Election date speculation

During the constitutional reform process in January 2026, analysts including Gaziz Abishev and Aidar Alibayev suggested that approval of the transition to a unicameral parliament would likely lead to early legislative elections rather than waiting until the scheduled 2028 vote, arguing that the authorities had incentives to complete the political transition before possible economic deterioration and declining public support.[^cite_note-95] Opposition politician [Amirjan Qosanov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amirjan_Qosanov) assessed that the constitutional referendum would be followed by parliamentary approval of the amendments by both chambers, after which elections to the unicameral Kurultai could be held within two to three months.[^cite_note-96]

In the referendum campaign period, Senate chairman [Mäulen Äşimbaev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C3%A4ulen_%C3%84%C5%9Fimbaev) stipulated that, if approved, the current bicameral Parliament would remain in office until 1 July 2026, after which elections to the new unicameral Kurultai would be held before 1 September 2026, when the new legislature would begin its duties.[^cite_note-97] Following the referendum, president Tokayev stated at a meeting with representatives of youth organizations involved in the campaign that elections to the Kurultai would be held in the summer.[^cite_note-98] He later confirmed in April 2026 that the vote would take place in the month of August,[^cite_note-99][^cite_note-100] a timeframe he reiterated again in early June.[^cite_note-101][^cite_note-102][^cite_note-103]

Political analyst Daniyar Ashimbayev stated that the constitutional transition created a compressed electoral timetable requiring elections to the Kurultai to be held between 1 August and 1 September 2026, noting media speculation surrounding possible election dates of 16 August, 23 August, and 30 August, while arguing that President Tokayev's advance announcement was intended to encourage parties to begin preparing for the campaign before the formal election decree was issued.[^cite_note-104] Following the [Amanat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amanat_\(political_party\)) party's announcement of its extraordinary congress in June 2026, he suggested that this was interpreted within broader election-date speculation under the same compressed timetable, with possible voting dates discussed as 16 or 23 August.[^cite_note-105]

On 30 June 2026, during an address to a joint session of Parliament, Tokayev announced that he would sign a decree calling elections to the Kurultai following the entry into force of the new Constitution, adding that the campaign should be conducted under conditions of open, honest, and fair political competition.[^cite_note-106][^cite_note-107][^cite_note-108]

### Transition to the Kurultai

On 1 July 2026, as the new constitution entered into force, replacing Kazakhstan's bicameral Parliament with the Kurultai as the country's unicameral legislature,[^cite_note-109][^cite_note-110] President [Kassym-Jomart Tokayev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kassym-Jomart_Tokayev) signed Decree No. 1338 calling the first elections to the Kurultai for 23 August 2026.[^cite_note-111][^cite_note-112] The decree instructed the [Central Election Commission](64644346.md) (CEC) to organize the election and directed the government and regional authorities to provide the necessary organizational, technical, and financial support.[^cite_note-election-date-kursiv-113]

In announcing the decree, Tokayev stated that the Kurultai should preserve Kazakhstan's parliamentary traditions while strengthening institutional stability and advancing the principles of a "Just Kazakhstan".[^cite_note-114][^cite_note-115]

## Electoral system

Following the adoption of a [new constitution](83368720.md) in the [March 2026 referendum](82396297.md), the electoral framework of Kazakhstan underwent its most significant restructuring since 1995. The reforms abolished the bicameral [Parliament of Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_Kazakhstan) by dissolving the upper chamber [Senate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senate_of_Kazakhstan) and established a [unicameral legislature](204304.md) known as the [Kurultai](83119061.md), whose members are known as [deputies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legislator).[^cite_note-116] The 145 deputies of the Kurultai are elected to five-year terms through a [closed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Closed_list) [party-list proportional representation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Party-list_proportional_representation) system from a single [nationwide constituency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/At-large), replacing the [mixed electoral system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mixed_electoral_system) used in the [2023 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Kazakh_legislative_election) and eliminating single-member constituencies.[^cite_note-117]

The reforms also marked a return to the nationwide [proportional representation](23298.md) system previously used between 2007 and 2021, for the first time since the [2021 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Kazakh_legislative_election).[^cite_note-Pelizzo-11] Political parties are required to surpass a 5% [electoral threshold](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_threshold) to obtain representation, with seats allocated through closed party lists using the [quota method](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quota_method). Under the constitution's retained imperative mandate provisions, deputies automatically lose their seats if they resign from or are expelled by their respective political parties.

### Electoral law amendments

Ahead of the 2026 legislative elections, the Parliament adopted amendments to the Constitutional Law "On Elections" and related legislation regulating online campaigning, campaign financing, and electoral transparency.[^cite_note-118] The amendments introduced administrative liability for online platforms that published campaign materials without the authorization of candidates or provided unequal conditions to candidates and political parties, while prohibiting financial and material support outside official election funds.[^cite_note-119][^cite_note-120][^cite_note-121] They also revised candidate nomination procedures, election administration, and voting rules, including provisions on reorganized election commissions, expanded access to polling stations, and safeguards allowing election results to be invalidated in cases of legal violations.[^cite_note-122]

During parliamentary consideration of the legislation, the CEC stated that online political endorsements, including unpaid posts by bloggers or users displaying party symbols, would require written authorization from the relevant candidate or political party, with unauthorized promotional content potentially constituting "hidden campaigning" under the Administrative Code and subject to administrative fines.[^cite_note-123][^cite_note-124] Human rights organizations and media observers criticized the proposed rules, arguing that their broad definition of online campaigning risked restricting independent political expression and online discourse ahead of the elections.[^cite_note-125][^cite_note-126] Kazakh officials maintained that the amendments were intended to strengthen transparency, regulate online campaigning, and ensure equal conditions among participants in the electoral process.[^cite_note-127][^cite_note-128]

## Electoral timetable



A "Don't Be Left Out! Vote!" Kazakh-language [graffiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Graffiti) on the ground calling for the electorate to vote in the election, [Shymkent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shymkent), Kazakhstan



A "Your Vote Matters" Russian-language graffiti for the election, [Almaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almaty), Kazakhstan

On 1 July 2026, following the signing of a presidential decree on the holding of elections, the (CEC adopted Resolution No. 26/41 "On approval of the Calendar Plan of the main events for the preparation and conduct of the elections of deputies of the Kurultai of the Republic of Kazakhstan, scheduled for 23 August 2026".[^cite_note-129] The calendar plan set out the main stages of the electoral process as follows:[^cite_note-130][^cite_note-131]

- 1 July 2026: Appointment of elections

- 2–13 July 2026 (until 18\:00 [UTC+5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UTC+05\:00)): Submission of party lists

- until 18\:00 on 23 July 2026: Registration of party lists

- until 11 July 2026: Announcement of the composition of territorial election commissions and their locations

- until 16 July 2026: Announcement of the composition of election commissions, as well as the boundaries of polling stations and locations of election commissions

- 23 July (18\:01 UTC+5) – 22 August 2026 (00\:00 UTC+5): Pre-election campaigning

- 2 August 2026: Submission of voter lists to precinct election commissions

- from 7 August 2026 – Voters' access to verify their personal data in the voter lists

- 22 August 2026 – [Election silence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Election_silence)

- 23 August 2026 – [Election day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Election_day)

## Political parties

As of 1 June 2026, eight political parties were registered in Kazakhstan, including the newly formed [Ädilet](83490988.md) party,[^cite_note-132][^cite_note-133] alongside [Amanat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amanat_\(political_party\)), [Auyl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auyl), [Aq Jol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aq_Jol), the [People's Party of Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Party_of_Kazakhstan), the [Nationwide Social Democratic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationwide_Social_Democratic_Party), [Baytaq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baytaq), and [Respublica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Respublica_\(Kazakh_political_party\)).[^cite_note-134] On 12 June, the ruling Amanat party announced its merger with Ädilet.

Justice Minister [Erlan Särsembaev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erlan_S%C3%A4rsembaev) stated that political party registration and reorganization procedures would continue until the new [constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1995_Constitution_of_Kazakhstan) entered into force on 1 July 2026, after which elections to the [Kurultai](83119061.md) would be announced. He added that new parties registered before that date would be eligible to participate in the 2026 elections.[^cite_note-135]

### Contesting parties

The CEC confirmed on 1 July 2026 that Ädilet, Auyl, Aq Jol, the People's Party of Kazakhstan, the Nationwide Social Democratic Party, Baytaq, and Respublica would participate in the elections, while Amanat did not contest the election following its merger into Ädilet.[^cite_note-136][^cite_note-137][^cite_note-138] On 13 July 2026, the nomination of candidates concluded, with the CEC receiving party lists containing a total of 546 candidates from the seven contesting political parties.[^cite_note-139][^cite_note-140]

On 23 July 2026, the CEC announced that it had registered 545 candidates for the 145 seats in the Kurultai, with all seven parties meeting the statutory quotas for women, youth, and persons with disabilities, which together comprised 43.9% of all candidates, including 172 women, 74 candidates under the age of 35, and 19 persons with disabilities, while the average candidate age was 45.4 years.[^cite_note-141] During the same briefing, the CEC conducted a draw, overseen by deputy chairman [Muhtar Erman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhtar_Erman?action=edit\&redlink=1), to determine the order in which the seven participating political parties would appear on the 2026 legislative election ballot as follows:[^cite_note-142][^cite_note-143]

## Campaign



Information board containing the various [electoral campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_campaign) posters of the contesting parties, [Almaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almaty), Kazakhstan, August 2026

### Campaign finance

On 1 July 2026, the CEC announced that the maximum size of political parties' election funds had been tripled under the new electoral legislation, increasing the maximum contribution from parties' own funds to 1.275 billion tenge and the ceiling for voluntary donations to 2.55 billion tenge, while maintaining that campaigns could be financed only through official election funds in order to ensure transparency and oversight of funding sources.[^cite_note-155]

On 15 July 2026, it was reported that political parties were required to pay an election deposit of 15 minimum wages for each candidate on their party lists, although parties that had received at least 5% of the vote in the [2023 legislative elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Kazakh_legislative_election) were exempt and those receiving between 3% and 5% paid half the standard amount, leaving only [Ädilet](83490988.md) and [Baytaq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baytaq) required to pay the full deposit.[^cite_note-156]

### Party campaigns

#### Ädilet

On 3 July 2026, the [Ädilet](83490988.md) party announced its readiness to contest the Kurultai elections.[^cite_note-157] Party chairman [Aibek Dädebai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aibek_D%C3%A4debai) said Ädilet would contest the election with a "clear program" and a professional team, describing the vote as an important stage in implementing the new constitution and strengthening Kazakhstan's democratic institutions. He stated that the party's campaign would focus on justice, the rule of law, responsibility, patriotism, and development, while pledging to compete in an open and lawful election campaign.[^cite_note-158][^cite_note-159]

On 10 July 2026, Ädilet held its 3rd Extraordinary Congress in Astana, where delegates approved the party's election programme, amended its charter, and nominated 186 candidates for the Kurultai elections.[^cite_note-160][^cite_note-161] Dädebai described the elections as an important stage in implementing the new constitution, stating that Ädilet's mission was to translate constitutional principles of justice, the rule of law, human dignity, and responsible governance into concrete policies and reforms.[^cite_note-162][^cite_note-Батухан-163] He presented the party's program as a five-year action plan focused on improving citizens' quality of life and strengthening cooperation between the state and society.[^cite_note-\:14-164] The party's platform emphasized political and institutional reforms, including strengthening democratic institutions, increasing electoral transparency, expanding citizen participation in decision-making, developing local self-government, improving budget transparency, and supporting judicial reform and civil society.[^cite_note-165] Its economic and social proposals included support for domestic production, the creation of one million quality jobs, expanded access to housing, improvements in education and healthcare, and the development of artificial intelligence skills among citizens.[^cite_note-\:14-164][^cite_note-Батухан-163] Digitalization was presented as a central priority, with party representative [Bağdat Musin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ba%C4%9Fdat_Musin) calling for wider use of digital technologies to improve public services, reduce bureaucracy, increase transparency, and prevent corruption, while also expanding rural internet access and AI-related initiatives.[^cite_note-\:14-164][^cite_note-166] In regional policy, Ädilet proposed reducing dependence on central government funding, expanding the powers of local representative bodies, and allocating at least 1 billion tenge annually in each region for youth mortgage programs while supporting young entrepreneurs through grants and employment initiatives.[^cite_note-167][^cite_note-168] The approved candidate list included Ädilet representatives, public figures, business representatives, and former Amanat members, including several former [Mäjilis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C3%A4jilis) deputies and prosecutor Aijan Aimaganova, who gained national prominence as the lead state prosecutor in the 2024 murder trial of former minister [Kuandyk Bishimbayev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuandyk_Bishimbayev).[^cite_note-169][^cite_note-170][^cite_note-171]

#### Aq Jol

On 10 June 2026, [Aq Jol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aq_Jol) chairman [Azat Peruaşev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azat_Perua%C5%9Fev) stated that the party intended to participate in the Kurultai elections and ruling out a potential merger with other political parties, adding that no such proposals had been received.[^cite_note-172]

On 5 July 2026, Aq Jol held its extraordinary election congress in [Astana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astana), where delegates approved the party's election programme, "Changes are Needed", and nominated 63 candidates.[^cite_note-173][^cite_note-\:6-174] The programme called for stable economic growth, fair competition, effective public administration, support for entrepreneurship, stronger parliamentarism, anti-corruption measures, technological development, and the protection of national interests.[^cite_note-\:6-174][^cite_note-\:7-175] Party chair [Dania Espaeva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dania_Espaeva) stated that the candidate list consisted of long-serving party members and professionals from business, academia, and public service, reflecting the party's emphasis on professional legislative experience.[^cite_note-\:6-174][^cite_note-\:7-175] Delegates also highlighted Aq Jol's parliamentary record since 2023, arguing that a number of the party's proposals on entrepreneurship, taxation, digital development, and parliamentary oversight had been incorporated into legislation and government policy.[^cite_note-\:7-175] During the congress, party representatives responded to criticism made at the previous day's [Respublica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Respublica_\(Kazakh_political_party\)) congress by defending Aq Jol's record of representing entrepreneurs and criticizing unnamed political rivals for relying on publicity rather than legislative achievements.[^cite_note-176][^cite_note-177][^cite_note-178]

#### Auyl

On 24 June 2026, during an extraordinary party congress, outgoing chairman [Serik Egizbaev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serik_Egizbaev) stated that his decision to step down from [Auyl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auyl) chairmanship was part of the party's "intention to fully participate in the upcoming elections under the new political system".[^cite_note-179][^cite_note-180]

At its 26th Extraordinary Congress on 6 July 2026, Auyl approved its election programme and nominated 69 candidates for the Kurultai elections.[^cite_note-181][^cite_note-182][^cite_note-183] The programme consisted of four sections and seven priority areas, focusing on regional development, food security, infrastructure, social policy, education and science, environmental protection, and support for traditional family values.[^cite_note-184][^cite_note-\:4-185] Campaigning under the slogan "Strong regions – strong center", the party stated that its programme was based on more than 75,000 proposals collected from across Kazakhstan through a digital platform.[^cite_note-\:4-185] According to First Deputy Chairman [Serik Egizbaev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serik_Egizbaev), the party sought to campaign on specific policy commitments and highlight its parliamentary record since 2023 rather than relying on broad campaign slogans.[^cite_note-186]

#### Baytaq

On 8 July 2026, [Baytaq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baytaq) held its 5th Extraordinary Congress in Astana, where delegates adopted the party's updated election platform, "Kazakhstan is a comfortable country for life!", approved amendments to the party charter, and nominated 51 candidates for the Kurultai elections.[^cite_note-187][^cite_note-188] The platform presented environmental protection as a central direction of state policy, linking it to economic development, healthcare, education, urban planning, and public administration, while proposing public monitoring of the party's election commitments.[^cite_note-189] Party chairman [Azamathan Ämirtai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azamathan_%C3%84mirtai) stated the new constitution marked a new stage in Kazakhstan's political development and that Baytaq would contest the elections with a platform centered on environmental protection, clean water, public health, and ESG-oriented projects, while announcing that the party's regional campaign headquarters would begin full-scale operations.[^cite_note-190][^cite_note-191]

The congress was also marked by internal disagreements, as political council member Asqar Imanliev criticized the election platform for insufficiently emphasizing environmental issues and called for a vote on re-electing party chairman Ämirtai, though the proposal was not adopted and the congress proceeded with approving the platform and candidate list.[^cite_note-192][^cite_note-193][^cite_note-194] On 13 July, Baytaq held a 6th Extraordinary Congress after four candidates voluntarily withdrew from the party list, reducing the number of nominees from 51 to 47 and requiring delegates to approve an updated electoral list before it was submitted to the CEC.[^cite_note-195][^cite_note-196]

#### JSDP

On 27 March 2026, the [Nationwide Social Democratic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationwide_Social_Democratic_Party) (JSDP) held an assembly in Astana following the [constitutional referendum](82396297.md), described by *BES.media* as a "pre-election review", where it presented its programme manifesto *The White Rose of Creation* and outlined policy priorities including social justice, economic redistribution, land reform, labor rights, environmental policy, and expanded social guarantees, positioning itself for the upcoming political cycle ahead of the 2026 legislative elections.[^cite_note-197][^cite_note-198]

The 25th JSDP Extraordinary Congress was held 8 July 2026 in Astana, where delegates approved the party's election programme, adopted a revised charter, renewed the political council, and approved a list of 33 candidates for the Kurultai elections. The party presented its programme, titled "20 Peaks on the Path of Progress", as a continuation of its two decades of social-democratic politics, focusing on freedom, justice, solidarity, democratization, economic reform, social protection, and public accountability.[^cite_note-\:11-199][^cite_note-\:13-200][^cite_note-201] JSDP chairman [Ashat Raqymjanov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashat_Raqymjanov) stated that the new constitution had created a new political environment and that the party would participate in the elections by promoting reforms aimed at strengthening political competition, local self-government, judicial independence, and citizens' constitutional rights.[^cite_note-\:11-199][^cite_note-\:13-200]

During the congress, the party emphasized policies including the demonopolization of the economy, anti-corruption measures, transparent public finances, protection of workers' rights, increased investment in education and healthcare, and the fair distribution of natural resource revenues.[^cite_note-\:11-199] The party's programme also proposed a phased transition to a [four-day working week](18573525.md) without reducing workers' incomes,[^cite_note-202] the redistribution of agricultural land to citizens for long-term targeted use,[^cite_note-203] and the restructuring of the [Samruk-Kazyna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samruk-Kazyna) sovereign wealth fund by transferring its functions to state ministries.[^cite_note-204]

JSDP representatives also argued that digitalization should serve not only technological modernization but also greater transparency in government decision-making, public spending, and accountability.[^cite_note-205] The approved candidate list included party leaders and current deputies, including Raqymjanov, [Nurlan Äuesbaev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nurlan_%C3%84uesbaev), Ajar Sagyndyqova, and Nauryz Sailaubai, while Raqymjanov stated that the party expected to secure at least 10% of the vote.[^cite_note-206] Party representatives also criticized competing parties, arguing that some political forces represented business interests rather than social-democratic principles.[^cite_note-207][^cite_note-208]

Following the submission of the JSDP's party list to the CEC, media reported that candidate Nurym Nurlybekov, who had previously faced criticism over anti-[LGBT](78174325.md) social media remarks in 2025, remained on the list.[^cite_note-\:16-209] Raqymjanov said the remarks did not reflect the party's position and that Nurlybekov had received a formal warning but remained a registered candidate.[^cite_note-\:16-209]

#### QHP

On 7 July 2026, the [People's Party of Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Party_of_Kazakhstan) (QHP) held its 25th Extraordinary Congress in Astana, where it approved its election programme and nominated 72 candidates for the first elections to the deputies of Kurultai.[^cite_note-\:8-210][^cite_note-211][^cite_note-\:9-212] The congress was the first party meeting following the entry into force of the 2026 Constitution and took place shortly after [Nursultan Şoqanov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nursultan_%C5%9Eoqanov) became party chairman following the resignation of [Ermukhamet Ertisbaev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ermukhamet_Ertisbaev).[^cite_note-\:9-212][^cite_note-213] The party's programme focused on social policy, labour rights, education, healthcare, regional development, and improving living standards, while its candidate list included representatives from business, law, education, medicine, culture, and public organizations.[^cite_note-\:8-210][^cite_note-214]

During the campaign, Şoqanov presented QHP as a party combining economic efficiency with social responsibility and rejected accusations that its platform represented simple state-funded populism.[^cite_note-\:10-215] He also highlighted the record of QHP's parliamentary faction during the [8th convocation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/8th_Parliament_of_Kazakhstan), citing legislative initiatives, amendments, and government inquiries as evidence of the party's work on behalf of citizens.[^cite_note-\:8-210][^cite_note-\:10-215]

#### Respublica

In early May 2026, [Majilis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C3%A4jilis) deputy and [Respublica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Respublica_\(Kazakh_political_party\)) party chairman [Aidarbek Qojanazarov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aidarbek_Qojanazarov) stated that hundreds of party members had expressed interest in becoming candidates for the Kurultai, adding that party list selection would be based on clear policy agendas rather than personal ambition, and that the Respublica party aimed to maximize its parliamentary representation depending on voter support.[^cite_note-216]

At its 5th Extraordinary Congress on 4 July 2026, Respublica re-elected Qojanazarov as its party chairman and approved its election programme and nominated 76 candidates for the 2026 elections.[^cite_note-217][^cite_note-218][^cite_note-219] The programme, adopted under the slogan "Citizen's Welfare – Responsible Society – Effective State – Safe Country", emphasized support for entrepreneurship, job creation, public administration reform, education and healthcare, digitalization, strengthening legal institutions, human capital development, and public safety.[^cite_note-\:5-220] During the congress, deputy chairman Oljas Quspekov criticized unnamed political forces that presented themselves as defenders of business interests, arguing that their political agenda had lost relevance, with his remarks, including a comparison of such forces to a "decorative pony", interpreted as criticism of the [Aq Jol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aq_Jol) party.[^cite_note-221][^cite_note-222][^cite_note-223] Qojanazarov stated that the party sought to promote economic modernization, support new industries, and strengthen fair competition.[^cite_note-\:5-220]

### Pre-election debates

On 23 July 2026, the CEC announced that pre-election debates would be held during the campaign period, with negotiations underway to organize them on the [24KZ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/24KZ) television channel, while political parties were also permitted to finance additional debates on other broadcasters through their election funds.[^cite_note-224]

On 29 July 2026, following the state broadcaster [Qazaqstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qazaqstan_\(TV_channel\))'s announcement the previous day, representatives of the seven contesting political parties participated in a nationally televised debate organized under the theme "The Future of Kazakhstan is Human Capital", signing a Charter of Ethics committing to refrain from personal attacks before debating their policies on education, employment, social welfare, regional development, environmental protection, entrepreneurship, and technological innovation,[^cite_note-225] while viewers were invited to submit questions through social media and [WhatsApp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WhatsApp) and speaking order was determined by random draw to ensure equal conditions for all participants.[^cite_note-226] Additionally, the pre-election debate was conducted entirely in the [Kazakh language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakh_language), marking the first nationally televised election debate in Kazakhstan to be held exclusively in Kazakh.[^cite_note-227] An alleged technical error occurred during the broadcast when the audience poll conducted during the debate, asking viewers to indicate which party representative they preferred, displayed incorrect figures totaling 156%; the moderator acknowledged the mistake during the live broadcast, after which corrected results were displayed.[^cite_note-228]

On 4 August 2026, Channel Seven held an open draw to determine the speaking order for the second televised debate, with representatives of all seven participating parties selecting numbered cards in a procedure conducted publicly to ensure equal conditions for the participants.[^cite_note-229] On 5 August, representatives of the seven parties participated in the second televised debate, which consisted of four stages covering the presentation of election programmes, proposals for the development of education, questions between party representatives, questions from viewers, and concluding statements.[^cite_note-230][^cite_note-231] The discussion focused on education, digitalization and artificial intelligence, higher education, vocational training, environmental education, teacher remuneration, support for rural specialists, and the development of science and technology.[^cite_note-232] Representatives of [Ädilet](83490988.md) proposed expanding the use of artificial intelligence in education and training five million Kazakhstanis in digital technologies and AI skills over five years, while [Respublica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Respublica_\(Kazakh_political_party\)) advocated reforms to higher education and greater international competitiveness; other parties addressed access to higher education, environmental education, vocational training, teacher salaries, rural employment, and scientific and technological development.[^cite_note-233][^cite_note-234][^cite_note-235]

On 12 August 2026, [Jibek Joly TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jibek_Joly_TV) held a nationally televised team debate, the first of its kind in Kazakhstan, featuring two representatives simultaneously from each of the seven contesting political parties, under the theme "Law and Order — the Basis of a Just Kazakhstan".[^cite_note-236] The debate consisted of four stages covering the presentation and criticism of party programmes, questions from journalists and viewers, direct exchanges between party representatives, and final "Promise" statements outlining commitments to voters.[^cite_note-237] The discussion addressed issues including judicial independence, law enforcement, alimony enforcement, gambling addiction, cattle theft, economic crime, vandalism, environmental responsibility, poverty, education, healthcare, and the protection of private property.[^cite_note-238][^cite_note-239][^cite_note-240]
