## Conduct





Voting ballot used in the election (written in both Kazakh and Russian)

[Elections in Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elections_in_Kazakhstan) are prepared and conducted by various bodies of election commissions.[^cite_note-248]

### Preparations



Youth volunteers in [Almaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almaty) distributing flyers for the election, August 2026

At its first meeting following the promulgation of the election decree on 1 July 2026, the CEC approved the election calendar, adopted implementing resolutions governing party nominations, candidate registration, campaigning, election observation, and voting procedures, established the administrative framework for organizing the first Kurultai elections under the new constitution, and launched a three-stage nationwide training programme for more than 71,000 election commission members to ensure the uniform application of electoral legislation.[^cite_note-249] As part of the programme, the CEC held training seminars for representatives of political parties on 3 July,[^cite_note-250] territorial election commissions on 17 July,[^cite_note-251] media representatives on 22 July,[^cite_note-252] and political parties, accredited public associations, and non-profit organizations on 28 July, covering electoral legislation, election administration, campaigning, financing, media coverage, and election observation.[^cite_note-253]

On 1 July, the CEC also announced that 10,427 polling stations would operate for the election, including 9,780 stations at voters' places of residence and 647 at places of temporary stay, with 82 stations to operate abroad in 64 countries; 71,266 election commission members were expected to participate in administering the vote, including 1,729 members of territorial commissions and 69,537 members of precinct commissions.[^cite_note-254] The [Ministry of Foreign Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Foreign_Affairs_\(Kazakhstan\)) subsequently on 23 July 2026 confirmed the establishment of 82 precinct election commissions at Kazakhstan's diplomatic missions abroad in 64 countries.[^cite_note-255] On 3 August 2026, precinct election commissions began operating, with more than 10,400 polling stations opening ahead of election day.[^cite_note-256][^cite_note-257]

On 3 August 2026, the CEC presented the ballot paper, which was to be printed double-sided on [A4 paper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_standard_paper_sizes), with the political parties listed in the order determined by the 23 July drawing followed by an "[Against all](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/None_of_the_above)" option;[^cite_note-258] 12,731,847 paper ballots in-total were to be produced for the 12,605,788 registered voters, including a 1% reserve.[^cite_note-259][^cite_note-260][^cite_note-261]

On 7 August 2026, the CEC launched online services enabling voters to determine the number and location of their assigned polling station by selecting their region and entering their individual identification number, with the service also made available through the eGov Mobile application.[^cite_note-262]

By 12 August 2026, the CEC reported that 10,423 polling stations would operate on election day, including 107 stations scheduled to open at 06\:00 rather than 07\:00 to serve voters at military units, inpatient medical institutions, continuously operating organizations, rotational workplaces, and special temporary-isolation facilities.[^cite_note-263][^cite_note-264]

On 13 August 2026, President [Kassym-Jomart Tokayev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kassym-Jomart_Tokayev) received CEC chairman [Nurlan Äbdirov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nurlan_%C3%84bdirov) for a briefing on preparations for the election and called for it to be held under the principle of "Law and Order" in an open and equal electoral environment.[^cite_note-265][^cite_note-266][^cite_note-267]

On 14 August 2026, the CEC launched Sailau AI, an AI-powered assistant available through its website and [Telegram](41501305.md) that provides voters with information in Kazakh and Russian on voting procedures, absentee certificates, voter-list issues, and other election-related matters, using the AlemLLM language model and relevant electoral legislation as its knowledge base.[^cite_note-268][^cite_note-269]

From 4 to 17 August, the CEC conducted inspection visits across all 20 regions to assess the readiness and material and technical equipment of polling stations, voter-information arrangements, call centres, and territorial election commissions, including the training of election commission members.[^cite_note-270]

On 18 August 2026, the Ministry of Foreign Affairs announced that three polling stations abroad—in [Ukraine](31750.md), [Iran](14653.md), and [Ethiopia](187749.md)—would not operate on election day because of the security situation in Ukraine and Iran and the lack of registered voters in Ethiopia, reducing the number of operational overseas polling stations from 82 to 79.[^cite_note-271][^cite_note-272]

As of 21 August 2026, the CEC had received 203 applications during the campaign, including 129 on electoral legislation and 62 on organizational matters, while reporting no complaints against territorial election commissions and warning that electoral interference, breaches of voting secrecy, obstruction of voting, and voting on behalf of others could incur liability.[^cite_note-273]

### Election funding

In May 2026, the CEC estimated that the cost of the 2026 elections could exceed 15–20 billion tenge, surpassing the expenses of previous national elections, including the [2026 constitutional referendum](82396297.md).[^cite_note-274][^cite_note-275] CEC Secretary [Şavkat Ötemisov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%9Eavkat_%C3%96temisov?action=edit\&redlink=1) stated that the final budget would be determined after the announcement of the election date, with major expenditures expected to be driven by the remuneration of election commission staff and logistical costs.[^cite_note-276][^cite_note-277] Deputy Prime Minister and Minister of National Economy [Serik Jumanğarin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serik_Juman%C4%9Farin) stated that the elections would be financed from the republican budget, with detailed calculations to be prepared once the official date was confirmed.[^cite_note-278]

In June 2026, CEC officials reported that the final cost estimate had not yet been submitted, and that the government was awaiting updated calculations before allocating funds.[^cite_note-279][^cite_note-280]

On 1 July 2026, the CEC confirmed that the 2026 elections would be financed from the government of Kazakhstan's reserve, as they had not been included in the 2026 state budget, with approximately 75% of total expenditures allocated to the remuneration of election commission members, while the remainder would cover printing, logistics, transportation, and information support costs, and that the final budget would be approved by decision of the cabinet following submission of consolidated estimates by the CEC.[^cite_note-281][^cite_note-282][^cite_note-283]

On 9 July 2026, the government allocated 34.3 billion tenge from its reserve to finance the elections, a decision announced by the CEC on 14 July.[^cite_note-284][^cite_note-\:15-285] According to CEC member [Mikhail Bortnik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikhail_Bortnik), about 8.5 billion tenge was allocated for the work of election commissions, while the funding also covered election administration in Kazakhstan and abroad, election materials, logistics, and overseas voting, for which 124.5 million tenge was allocated to the [Ministry of Foreign Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Foreign_Affairs_\(Kazakhstan\)).[^cite_note-\:15-285][^cite_note-286][^cite_note-287]

### Electronic voting

CEC secretary Şavkat Ötemisov stated that [electronic voting](371301.md) was not under active consideration for the 2026 election, although Kazakhstan continued to study international experience with electronic voting systems. Ötemisov cited cybersecurity risks, including concerns over external interference and cyberattacks against election infrastructure, as well as the high projected costs of implementing a secure electronic voting system.[^cite_note-288] At the same time, Minister of Artificial Intelligence and Digital Development [Jaslan Mädiev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaslan_M%C3%A4diev) stated that secure electronic voting systems could be developed in Kazakhstan if formally required, noting that technical solutions could be designed to meet cybersecurity, speed, and usability requirements.[^cite_note-289]

### Media and information environment

On 9 July 2026, First Vice Minister of Culture and Information [Qanat Ysqaqov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qanat_Ysqaqov?action=edit\&redlink=1) announced that the ministry would monitor more than 20,000 websites and online platforms during the election campaign, compared with about 1,000 media outlets in previous elections. He stated that the expansion reflected the growth of social media and online disinformation and argued that ensuring citizens' access to reliable information was necessary for the public legitimacy of the elections.[^cite_note-290]

On 23 July 2026, Ysqaqov stated that media outlets could conduct election-related blitz polls, provided they were not presented as sociological surveys or accompanied by unauthorized publication of samples and final results, while the CEC clarified that formal public opinion polls could only be conducted by organizations registered with the commission under electoral law.[^cite_note-291] The Centre for Combating Disinformation of the Central Communications Service (JAQ.OrtCom) also warned of increased election-related disinformation, reporting fake social media pages impersonating a political party, coordinated bot activity targeting official campaign accounts, and the potential use of artificial intelligence to generate misleading election content, while urging voters to verify information before sharing it.[^cite_note-292]

Vice Minister of Artificial Intelligence and Digital Development Baqtiiar Muhametqaliev stated that digital platforms operating in Kazakhstan, including [TikTok](56822861.md) and [YouTube](3524766.md), were required to comply with national legislation governing AI-generated content, including mandatory labeling requirements, and warned that the growing use of artificial intelligence to create misinformation posed an increasing challenge during the election period.[^cite_note-293][^cite_note-294]

JAQ.OrtCom subsequently reported the circulation of fabricated statements, press releases, photographs, and videos using the identities of heads of state bodies and CEC representatives, while urging voters to rely on official sources and avoid sharing unverified information; it also said that the [Prosecutor General's Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prosecutor_General's_Office_of_Kazakhstan) was investigating cases involving the dissemination of false information.[^cite_note-295][^cite_note-296] On 11 August, the Legal Media Center expressed concern over what it described as increased pressure on freedom of expression, citing restrictions affecting independent media outlets and journalists and reporting that [Radio Free Europe/Radio Liberty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_Free_Europe/Radio_Liberty) journalists faced a risk of losing accreditation during the election period.[^cite_note-297] On 14 August, the [Committee to Protect Journalists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Committee_to_Protect_Journalists) joined eight local and international press-freedom and human-rights organizations in expressing concern over what they described as escalating pressure on independent journalists ahead of the election, citing the arrest of political commentator Azamat Omarov, criminal investigations and travel restrictions involving journalists Lukpan Akhmedyarov and Dinara Yegeubayeva, and the prison sentence imposed on reporter Aleksandra Alyokhova.[^cite_note-298] On 15 August 2026, sociologist Serik Beisembaev, director of the PaperLab research centre, told Radio Free Europe/Radio Liberty that the authorities had chosen to strengthen political control rather than pursue further liberalisation following the [January 2022 unrest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Kazakh_unrest), with pressure expanding from major media and political figures to journalists, bloggers, activists, and social-media users, contributing to increased self-censorship and a narrowing space for public criticism, while he also criticised the return to a fully [proportional electoral system](23298.md), arguing that the absence of self-nominated candidates reduced the independence of deputies and weakened political representation.[^cite_note-299]

On 14 August 2026, JAQ.OrtCom reported a further increase in election-related disinformation, identifying approximately 15 coordinated campaigns distributed through more than 200 anonymous and destructive accounts using [Telegram](41501305.md) channels, social media, bot networks, manipulated documents, and AI-generated audio, photographs and videos, including deepfakes featuring public figures and heads of state bodies, while noting that 44 counter-disinformation materials had been produced to debunk false claims and improve public information literacy.[^cite_note-300][^cite_note-301][^cite_note-302] JAQ.OrtCom's expert Aibar Oljai attributed the intensification of information manipulation to the heightened political importance of the election period and warned that such campaigns could be used to influence public opinion, while urging citizens to critically assess and verify information encountered online.[^cite_note-303]

On 21 August 2026, deputy prosecutor general Erlan Ötegenov reported that prosecutors had registered eight electoral law violations, including three related to public opinion polling and five to campaigning during the prohibited period, resulting in administrative liability for seven individuals and one legal entity.[^cite_note-304][^cite_note-305] Later that day, First Vice Minister of Culture and Information Qanat Ysqaqov reported that the ministry had identified 21 instances of materials allegedly violating electoral law during the campaign, most involving unauthorized public opinion polls or the publication of poll results during prohibited periods, while other cases concerned improper or missing labeling of election-related materials.[^cite_note-306][^cite_note-307][^cite_note-308]

### Voter registration

By 1 July and 1 January every year, information on voters and the boundaries of polling stations are submitted by the local executive bodies (*akimats*) in electronic form to their territorial election commissions, which ensure the verification and submission of information to the higher election commissions.[^cite_note-Қазақстан-309]

As of 1 July 2026, the CEC reported that 12,561,644 citizens were registered in the [voter list](1516039.md),[^cite_note-310][^cite_note-311] representing an increase of 99,848 voters since the [2026 referendum](82396297.md).[^cite_note-312] By 2 August 2026, the number of registered voters had increased to 12,605,788, with the largest voter populations recorded in [Almaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almaty) (1,329,251), [Turkistan Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkistan_Region) (more than one million), [Astana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astana) (1,014,817), [Karaganda Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karaganda_Region) (758,489), [Jambyl Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jambyl_Region) (730,236), and [Shymkent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shymkent) (713,491), while [Ulytau Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulytau_Region) had the smallest electorate, with 138,362 registered voters.[^cite_note-313][^cite_note-314][^cite_note-315]

Voter registration and related procedures followed the deadlines set by the Chapter 4 of the Constitutional Law "On the Republican Referendum" and the associated electoral regulations. Akimats are responsible for compiling the voter lists at the place of residence, updating them to reflect recent migration or submitted applications, and submitting them to the precinct and territorial referendum commissions.[^cite_note-Қазақстан-309] Voters wishing to vote at a polling station other than their place of permanent registration were required to submit a written application thirty days before election day (by 23 July 2026) to their local äkimat to be temporarily included in another polling station's voter list.[^cite_note-Қазақстан-309][^cite_note-\:17-316] Twenty days before election day (2 August), akimats were required to finalize the voter lists, electronically transfer them to the relevant precinct election commissions, and freeze further routine amendments except those permitted by law.[^cite_note-Қазақстан-309][^cite_note-317] On 3 August, the CEC also announced the introduction of online services enabling precinct election commissions to verify a citizen's permanent registration through the eGov portal and, in certain cases, allow temporary registration at the address of a polling station on election day for citizens who had been removed from registration and were not otherwise included in the voter lists; the registration-verification service was to be available at limited 5,106 polling stations.[^cite_note-318][^cite_note-319] Fifteen days before election day (7 August), the voter lists were made available for public inspection at polling stations, allowing voters to verify their registration and request corrections or additions to inaccurate or incomplete information before election day.[^cite_note-Қазақстан-309][^cite_note-\:17-316]

Voters who expected to be away from their registered polling station could obtain an [absentee voting certificate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Absentee_ballot) from their precinct election commission between the submission of voter lists for review and election day (23 August 2026), enabling them to vote at another polling station within Kazakhstan, provided they had not already been included in another voter list.[^cite_note-Қазақстан-309] By election day, 17,132 voters had received certificates of exclusion from the voter list granting them the right to vote at another polling station according to CEC deputy chairman.[^cite_note-320]

### Accessibility

On 14 July 2026, the CEC announced that polling stations would provide barrier-free access for voters with disabilities, together with Braille ballot templates, audio instructions, and sign language interpretation, while local authorities were instructed to provide accessible transportation and assess polling station accessibility in cooperation with disability organizations.[^cite_note-321][^cite_note-322][^cite_note-323] CEC chairman [Nurlan Äbdirov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nurlan_%C3%84bdirov) called on political parties to ensure that campaign materials and public events were accessible to voters with disabilities, including by providing sign language interpretation, accessible election programmes, and barrier-free access to campaign venues.[^cite_note-324][^cite_note-325][^cite_note-326]

On 11 August 2026, the CEC Working Group on Ensuring the Electoral Rights of Persons with Disabilities reviewed the accessibility of polling stations with the participation of more than 100 representatives of election commissions, government bodies, political parties, disability organizations, and election observation missions, assessing physical accessibility and the availability of assistive materials, while also reviewing the Interactive Accessibility Map and Braille and audio ballot materials and issuing recommendations to address identified shortcomings before election day.[^cite_note-327]

Ahead of election day, the Almaty City Akimat announced on 19 August 2026 that 170 Invataxi vehicles, including 36 specially equipped vehicles, would provide transportation for voters with disabilities and limited mobility, including wheelchair users and people with visual impairments.[^cite_note-328][^cite_note-329]

During election day, accessibility audit expert Almaz Utkurov reported that some polling stations in Almaty still had accessibility problems, including missing ramps, malfunctioning assistance call buttons, and delays in the provision of Invataxi transportation, although he found conditions at some polling stations to be generally compliant with accessibility requirements.[^cite_note-330]

### Election day

Blue-curtained voting booths stand in a row inside a school hallway

A half-full transparent ballot box with an emblem of Kazakhstan image stands on a carpet inside a school hallway

The voting booths (above) and ballot box (below) of the No. 490 polling station of [Astana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astana), Kazakhstan

On 23 August 2026, voting in the Kurultai elections began at 07\:00, with 10,363 polling stations operating across Kazakhstan and 19 polling stations in 16 foreign countries, while 107 polling stations, mainly at military units, had opened at 06\:00, with the first overseas polling stations opening in [Seoul](19159283.md) and [Tokyo](30057.md).[^cite_note-331]

On election day, online voter registration verification services were available at 5,106 polling stations, while one day temporary registration services operated at 221 polling stations, allowing eligible citizens without a current registration at their polling station to be added to the voter list and vote.[^cite_note-332]

From 09\:00,[^cite_note-333] the [Qazaqstan Radio and Television Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qazaqstan_Radio_and_Television_Corporation), with the support of national and regional media, conducted a 16-hour nationwide online marathon until 00\:30, featuring live coverage from Astana, Almaty and Shymkent, updates from overseas polling stations, briefings by the CEC and international observers, and interviews and discussions with public figures, experts, journalists, and civil society representatives.[^cite_note-334][^cite_note-335]

Election day publicity included parachutist Timofey Merkulov landing near a polling station in the Almaty Region after jumping from an aircraft,[^cite_note-336][^cite_note-337] while a family arrived at another polling station by hot air balloon.[^cite_note-338][^cite_note-339]

During voting, seven voters in Astana, Almaty, Shymkent, Oral and Kostanay were fined for publishing photos of completed ballots, the [Prosecutor General's Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prosecutor_General's_Office_of_Kazakhstan) reported, while authorities recorded no violations by election commissions or serious public-order incidents.[^cite_note-340][^cite_note-341][^cite_note-342]

Activists also reported restrictions on election day. Almaty-based civil activist Äset Äbişev said police had barricaded the entrance to his home, in which he described as a violation of his right to vote,[^cite_note-343] while North Kazakhstan activist Nurbol Önerhan, who was under probation and subject to electronic monitoring, said he was repeatedly required to report to probation officials on election day and that police later visited his home.[^cite_note-344]

Voting ended at 20\:00, after which polling stations closed and election commissions began counting the ballots.[^cite_note-345][^cite_note-346][^cite_note-347] Overseas polling stations operated according to local time zones, with the final polling station, in [San Francisco](49728.md), closing later than those in Kazakhstan.[^cite_note-348][^cite_note-349][^cite_note-350]

## Observation

Senate chairman [Mäulen Äşimbaev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C3%A4ulen_%C3%84%C5%9Fimbaev), during the council meeting of the [CIS Interparliamentary Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CIS_Interparliamentary_Assembly) (IPA CIS) on 21 May, invited observers from the IPA CIS to observe the elections and expressed hope for their participation.[^cite_note-351]

On 9 July 2026, the Kazakh government invited the Parliamentary Assembly of the Collective Security Treaty Organization (CSTO) and the CIS Interparliamentary Assembly to observe the Kurultai elections, with Foreign Minister [Ermek Köşerbaev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ermek_K%C3%B6%C5%9Ferbaev) stating that the vote would be conducted in accordance with national legislation and international standards.[^cite_note-352]

By 18 August 2026, the CEC had accredited a total of 777 international observers from 42 countries and 13 international organizations, including 138 members of parliament from 41 countries and representatives of the [OSCE Office for Democratic Institutions and Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Office_for_Democratic_Institutions_and_Human_Rights), [Commonwealth of Independent States](36870.md), [Shanghai Cooperation Organisation](152422.md), [CIS Interparliamentary Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CIS_Interparliamentary_Assembly), and [Turkic Academy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkic_Academy), while election authorities from 22 countries, including 11 foreign heads of CECs, were also expected to monitor the vote.[^cite_note-353][^cite_note-354][^cite_note-355]

Ahead of the vote, independent observers raised concerns over restrictions on domestic election monitoring, as amendments to the electoral law barred Kazakh organizations receiving foreign funding from conducting observation, affecting established groups such as Erkindik Qanaty and the Young Voters League, while other organizations reported administrative obstacles to accreditation.[^cite_note-356]

### OSCE

On 18 May 2026, the CEC met with a delegation of [Organization for Security and Co-operation in Europe](22796.md) (OSCE) Permanent Representatives to discuss constitutional and electoral reforms, measures to strengthen electoral infrastructure and rights protections, concerns over external interference, and future cooperation, with CEC deputy chairman [Muqtar Erman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muqtar_Erman?action=edit\&redlink=1) announcing that an official invitation would be sent to the OSCE [Office for Democratic Institutions and Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Office_for_Democratic_Institutions_and_Human_Rights) (ODIHR) to observe the upcoming Kurultai elections.[^cite_note-357]

On 10 July 2026, following an invitation from the Kazakh authorities, the ODIHR opened an election observation mission in [Astana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astana) led by ambassador Vincent Piket, comprising a 12-member core team, with 22 long-term observers scheduled to deploy from 18 July and up to 300 short-term observers requested to observe election day alongside delegations from the [OSCE Parliamentary Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OSCE_Parliamentary_Assembly) and the [Parliamentary Assembly of the Council of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_Assembly_of_the_Council_of_Europe).[^cite_note-358] On the same day, the mission met with the CEC, where both sides discussed preparations for the elections, the new electoral system, the work of election commissions, voter information, and the role of domestic and international observers, while the CEC stated it would provide the mission with the conditions necessary to carry out its work in accordance with Kazakhstan's legislation and international obligations.[^cite_note-359] On 23 July, Piket confirmed that 22 long-term observers had been deployed to Kazakhstan to monitor the election campaign and electoral process.[^cite_note-360][^cite_note-361]

On 13 August 2026, the OSCE Parliamentary Assembly announced that nearly 80 parliamentarians and staff from around 30 countries would deploy to Kazakhstan as short-term observers for the 23 August elections, led by OSCE Special Co-ordinator [Gábor Hajdu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G%C3%A1bor_Hajdu) of Romania and Head of the OSCE PA Delegation [Lucija Tacer Perlin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lucija_Tacer_Perlin) of Slovenia; the delegation would work alongside the OSCE/ODIHR mission and the PACE delegation, with observers receiving briefings from election authorities, political parties, civil society, media, and independent experts before deploying across Kazakhstan to assess the electoral process.[^cite_note-362]

On 7 August 2026, the OSCE/ODIHR election observation mission published an interim report assessing the electoral environment and preparations for the election. The report raised concerns regarding political pluralism, media freedom, and self-censorship, noting that all participating parties supported the president's policies and that some interlocutors considered the absence of genuine opposition parties and independent candidates a limitation on political participation and pluralism.[^cite_note-363] The mission also reported that pressure on dissidents contributed to self-censorship and that state ownership and non-transparent budget subsidies limited media pluralism despite the large number of registered media outlets.[^cite_note-364]

On 24 August 2026, the ODIHR, together with the OSCE PA and the Parliamentary Assembly of the Council of Europe, said the elections were technically well prepared but took place amid limited political pluralism, restrictions on independent candidates and media, an uneven playing field that advantaged the [Ädilet](83490988.md) party through the use of public resources, and reports of pressure on voters and restricted observer access to vote counting.[^cite_note-365] The observers also reported discrepancies between voter activity observed at polling stations and officially reported turnout figures, as well as significant procedural violations during the vote count, raising concerns about the integrity of turnout reporting and the counting process.[^cite_note-366]

## Opinion polls

As of 22 July 2026, the CEC had received notifications from six legal entities intending to conduct election-related public opinion polls — Institute of Eurasian Integration, DATAmetrics, Institute for Public Policy, Institute for Comprehensive Social Research–Astana (SOCIS-A), Kazakhstan Institute of Social Development, and Research Association "Institute of Democracy" — using methods including face-to-face, telephone and internet surveys, focus groups, expert surveys, and exit polls.[^cite_note-367]

Multiple opinion polls conducted during the 2026 election campaign reported relatively high declared voter participation. A comparative analysis published by the [Kazakhstan Institute for Strategic Studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakhstan_Institute_for_Strategic_Studies) on 17 August, based on seven surveys conducted between 13 June and 17 August, found that between 70.3% and 75.6% of respondents stated their intentions to vote, while the share of undecided voters declined substantially during the campaign, from 22.6% in a DATAMetrics survey conducted from 13 June to 2 July to 5.3% in the latest measurement by the Public Policy Institute. The surveys also indicated continued leadership by [Ädilet](83490988.md), followed by [Auyl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auyl) generally ranked second, while [Aq Jol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aq_Jol) and [Respublica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Respublica_\(Kazakh_political_party\)) competed for the following positions.[^cite_note-368][^cite_note-369][^cite_note-370]

### Exit polls

Exit polls released at 00\:00 [UTC+05\:00](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UTC+05\:00) on 24 August indicated that the support for [Ädilet](83490988.md) was projected to hover between 70% and 72%, while the [People's Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Party_of_Kazakhstan) and [Baytaq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baytaq) were projected to not enter the Kurultai.[^cite_note-381][^cite_note-382]

## Results

On 24 August 2026, the CEC announced preliminary results showing that [Ädilet](83490988.md) won 71.17% of the vote, followed by [Auyl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auyl) with 6.26%, [Respublica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Respublica_\(Kazakh_political_party\)) with 5.56%, [Aq Jol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aq_Jol) with 5.21%, and the [Nationwide Social Democratic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationwide_Social_Democratic_Party) (JSDP) with 5.11%, while the [People's Party of Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Party_of_Kazakhstan) (QHP) and [Baytaq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baytaq) received 3.11% and 1.07%, respectively, with turnout reaching 73.8% as 9,351,539 of 12,623,289 registered voters participated.[^cite_note-384][^cite_note-385][^cite_note-386] Five parties therefore cleared the 5% [electoral threshold](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_threshold) and were eligible for representation in the 145-seat [Kurultai](83119061.md),[^cite_note-387][^cite_note-388] while the QHP and Baytaq fell below the threshold.[^cite_note-389]

The CEC announced the final results on 25 August, confirming the preliminary results and allocating 110 seats to Ädilet, 10 to Auyl, nine to Respublica, and eight each to Aq Jol and the JSDP, while the QHP and Baytaq received no mandate after failing to cross the electoral threshold.[^cite_note-390][^cite_note-391] 74.1% (9,351,539) of registered voters participated in the election, about 224,000 more voters than in the [March 2026 constitutional referendum](82396297.md).[^cite_note-392] The result also marked the first absence of the left-wing QHP from the national legislature since it originally entered the lower house [Mäjılıs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C3%A4j%C4%B1l%C4%B1s) as the Communist People's Party of Kazakhstan in [2012](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Kazakh_legislative_election).[^cite_note-393]

### Voter turnout

Final turnout reached 74.19%, the highest turnout in a Kazakh legislative election since [2016](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Kazakh_legislative_election),[^cite_note-394] and exceeding the previous five-year record of 73.12% recorded at the [2026 referendum](82396297.md); turnout was highest in [Turkistan Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkistan_Region) (92.16%) and lowest in [Almaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almaty) (43.23%).[^cite_note-395]

According to the [Ministry of Foreign Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Foreign_Affairs_\(Kazakhstan\)), 9,623 Kazakh citizens voted at 79 polling stations abroad in 61 countries, representing 84.4% of registered overseas voters.[^cite_note-396][^cite_note-397]

The [Ministry of Culture and Information](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Culture_and_Information_\(Kazakhstan\)) later reported that around 4 million young people participated in the election, including first-time voters and those aged 25–35.[^cite_note-398]

## Aftermath

### Analysis

Political analysts and experts argued that the return to a nationwide [party-list proportional representation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Party-list_proportional_representation) system and the establishment of the unicameral [Kurultai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurultai) strengthened executive control and limited political competition. Political analyst Temur Umarov of the [Carnegie Russia Eurasia Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnegie_Endowment_for_International_Peace#Carnegie_Russia_Eurasia_Center) said the system gave the authorities greater control over candidate selection and made the Kurultai more manageable for the executive, while arguing that the election primarily served to preserve political stability and elite continuity rather than initiate major democratic reforms.[^cite_note-406] Luca Anceschi of the [University of Glasgow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Glasgow) similarly viewed the elections as contributing to the consolidation of Tokayev's power, while former senator [Zauresh Battalova](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zauresh_Battalova?action=edit\&redlink=1) criticized the limited scope for genuine political choice under the new system.[^cite_note-407] Stefania Kolyaj of the [Polish Institute of International Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_Institute_of_International_Affairs) likewise described the election as a formal consolidation of Tokayev's power and argued that the newly formed Kurultai would have little influence over state policy.[^cite_note-408] Polish political analyst Wojciech Górecki of the [Centre for Eastern Studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centre_for_Eastern_Studies) argued that the elections, despite their routine character, demonstrated the balance of power within Kazakhstan's ruling camp and confirmed the dominance of the presidential center of power, with the shift to a unicameral parliament potentially making the legislature easier for the executive to manage.[^cite_note-409] Catherine Putz of *[The Diplomat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Diplomat_\(magazine\))* similarly argued that the election represented institutional change without a corresponding shift in the balance of political power, pointing to [Ädilet](83490988.md)'s absorption of the former ruling [Amanat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amanat_\(political_party\)) party and the continued dominance of pro-presidential forces.[^cite_note-410] Political scientist Tolğanai Ümbetälieva of the Central Asian Foundation for the Development of Democracy similarly argued that, unlike the previous [8th Parliament of Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/8th_Parliament_of_Kazakhstan), which could at times oppose the president, the new Kurultai was effectively under presidential control.[^cite_note-411]

Political scientist Dosym Satbaev argued that the election offered limited political choice, with the participating parties largely pro-government, and questioned the credibility of the official turnout figures due to the lack of independent exit polls. He also linked the election to broader discussions over the future transfer of power and President Tokayev's possible decision to seek another presidential term before 2029.[^cite_note-412]

Political scientist Qazybek Maigeldinov attributed the [People's Party of Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Party_of_Kazakhstan)'s poor performance to increased competition among left-wing parties for socially oriented voters and the declining appeal of its Soviet-associated ideological platform, arguing that its [nostalgic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nostalgia_for_the_Soviet_Union) appeal was increasingly limited to older generations as [Gen Z](4015968.md) in Kazakhstan, with little connection to the country's communist past, developed different political views and orientations.[^cite_note-413]

Analysts also argued that the election would reinforce Kazakhstan's existing multi-vector foreign policy rather than produce a major shift in its external relations. Jennifer Brick Murtazashvili of the [Carnegie Endowment for International Peace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnegie_Endowment_for_International_Peace) described Tokayev's approach as "portfolio politics", involving the allocation of different roles to Russia, China, the European Union, and the United States, while noting that the strengthened presidential control could give the government greater latitude in managing these relationships.[^cite_note-414]

### Reactions

Tokayev praised the elections as "successfully and impeccably organized", thanking voters, observers, and the Central Election Commission for their role in the process.[^cite_note-415][^cite_note-416]

The [Baytaq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baytaq) party initially disputed the exit poll results and alleged violations at polling stations, saying it would seek to establish the "real results" through legal procedures. Party leader [Azamathan Ämirtai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azamathan_%C3%84mirtai) later asserted he had no confirmed evidence to question the voters' choice and would respect the official results established by law.[^cite_note-417][^cite_note-418] Representatives of the party's West Kazakhstan, Karaganda, and Aktobe branches likewise said they respected the voters' choice and accepted the preliminary results, while the latter two called for an internal review of the party's campaign and organizational methods.[^cite_note-419][^cite_note-420]

Following its electoral defeat and failure to win any seats, the [People's Party of Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Party_of_Kazakhstan) (QHP) chairman [Nursultan Şoqanov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nursultan_%C5%9Eoqanov) recognized the results as objective and said the party was ready to work constructively with other political forces and continue its political activities.[^cite_note-421][^cite_note-422] Former party chairman and adviser [Ermukhamet Ertisbaev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ermukhamet_Ertisbaev), a longtime senior political figure who had resigned as QHP chairman in June, admitted the party's programme of "Kazakhstani socialism" had failed to gain sufficient mass support and announced his retirement from active politics, saying he would focus on international affairs and education.[^cite_note-423][^cite_note-424][^cite_note-425][^cite_note-426]

#### International reactions

**Countries**

-  [Russia](25391.md)**:** President [Vladimir Putin](32817.md) congratulated Tokayev on the election and said the turnout and results demonstrated public support for the course pursued under Tokayev's leadership toward Kazakhstan's development, greater internal political stability and economic progress.

  [^cite_note-427]

-  [Uzbekistan](31853.md)**:** President [Shavkat Mirziyoyev](399855.md) congratulated Tokayev on the successful first election to the unicameral Kurultai and expressed confidence that the new legislature would advance Kazakhstan's reforms, provide legislative support for major transformations and give new impetus to sustainable development.

  [^cite_note-428]

-  [Azerbaijan](746.md)**:** President [Ilham Aliyev](22765442.md) congratulated Tokayev and the Kazakh people on the successful election, describing it as the beginning of a new historical chapter in Kazakhstan's sociopolitical life and expressing confidence that it would support the country's development and continuation of Tokayev's reforms.

  [^cite_note-429]

-  [Kyrgyzstan](170131.md)**:** President [Sadyr Japarov](65513403.md) congratulated Tokayev and the Kazakh people, saying that the newly elected Kurultai had the potential to strengthen Kazakhstan's statehood and develop its sociopolitical and legal institutions.

  [^cite_note-430]

-  [Tajikistan](30108.md)**:** President [Emomali Rahmon](393634.md) congratulated Tokayev on the successful conduct of the Kurultai elections and emphasized the importance of continued Tajikistan–Kazakhstan cooperation and the development of bilateral relations.

  [^cite_note-431]

-  [Belarus](3457.md)**:** President [Alexander Lukashenko](156589.md) congratulated Tokayev on the election and expressed confidence in the continued development of relations between Belarus and Kazakhstan.

  [^cite_note-432]

-  [Armenia](10918072.md)**:** Prime Minister [Nikol Pashinyan](17897961.md) congratulated Tokayev on the successful election and said the convincing results demonstrated broad public support for Tokayev's reform course.

  [^cite_note-433]

-  [Turkmenistan](198149.md)**:** President [Serdar Berdimuhamedov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serdar_Berdimuhamedow) congratulated Tokayev on the successful election and described the first Kurultai election as an important step in implementing reforms aimed at improving Kazakhstan's system of state power and governance.

  [^cite_note-434]

-  [Turkey](11125639.md)**:** President [Recep Tayyip Erdoğan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recep_Tayyip_Erdo%C4%9Fan) congratulated Tokayev and said the high voter turnout was an indication of public support for the reforms being pursued in Kazakhstan.

  [^cite_note-435]

  [^cite_note-436]

-  [Georgia](48768.md)**:** Prime Minister [Irakli Kobakhidze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irakli_Kobakhidze) congratulated Tokayev and the Kazakh people on the first Kurultai election and expressed confidence that the election would contribute to Kazakhstan's continued development, stability, prosperity and the well-being of its people.

  [^cite_note-\:12-437]

  [^cite_note-438]

-  [Serbia](29265.md)**:** President [Aleksandar Vučić](13349267.md) congratulated Tokayev on the successful election and highlighted strong public support for his reform agenda.

  [^cite_note-439]

  [^cite_note-440]

-  [Pakistan](23235.md)**:** President [Asif Ali Zardari](993655.md) congratulated Tokayev and the Kazakh people on the successful completion of the Kurultai election, commended public participation in the political process, and expressed his best wishes for the newly elected legislature.

  [^cite_note-441]

-  [United Arab Emirates](69328.md)**:** President [Mohamed bin Zayed Al Nahyan](2121945.md) congratulated Tokayev on the successful election and conveyed his wishes for Kazakhstan's continued development and prosperity.

  [^cite_note-442]

-  [Israel](9282173.md)**:** Prime Minister [Benjamin Netanyahu](56472.md) congratulated Tokayev on the election and expressed his wishes for Kazakhstan's continued stability and development.

  [^cite_note-\:12-437]

-  [Myanmar](19457.md)**:** President [Min Aung Hlaing](38278640.md) congratulated Tokayev on the successful conduct of the Kurultai election and conveyed his wishes for Kazakhstan's continued development.

  [^cite_note-443]

**International organizations**

-  [European Union](9317.md)**:** The EU welcomed the election's orderly organization and participation while noting OSCE/ODIHR concerns over limited political and media pluralism, electoral shortcomings, voting and counting violations, and discrepancies in reported turnout, and urged Kazakhstan to continue democratic reforms concerning human rights, the rule of law and democratic governance.

  [^cite_note-444]

  [^cite_note-445]

-  [Commonwealth of Independent States](36870.md): The CIS observer mission characterized the election as well organized, fair, competitive and open, stating that it enabled citizens to freely express their will and that observers were provided with conditions to work in accordance with international standards.

  [^cite_note-446]

  [^cite_note-447]

### Opening session of the 1st Kurultai

On 26 August 2026, Tokayev signed a decree convening the first session of the Kurultai of the [first convocation](83747507.md) for 28 August.[^cite_note-448][^cite_note-449][^cite_note-450] On 27 August, the CEC registered the newly 145 elected deputies, formally beginning their mandate powers.[^cite_note-451][^cite_note-452]

During the first opening session, Tokayev outlined the Kurultai's priorities, including updating legislation to conform with the new constitution and ensuring the stability and quality of lawmaking following the abolition of the [Senate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senate_of_Kazakhstan).[^cite_note-453][^cite_note-454][^cite_note-455] He also called on the Kurultai to prioritize main legislation proposed by the government that would prohibit Kazakh children under 16 from registering on social media platforms.[^cite_note-456][^cite_note-457] Tokayev nominated [Aibek Dädebai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aibek_D%C3%A4debai), leader of [Ädilet](83490988.md), as [chairman of the Kurultai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chairman_of_the_Kurultai),[^cite_note-458] and 144 deputies subsequently [elected](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_Chairman_of_the_Kurultai_election) him unanimously by secret ballot.[^cite_note-459][^cite_note-460][^cite_note-461] The Kurultai then approved three [deputy chairmen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deputy_Chairman_of_the_Kurultai) and established eight standing parliamentary committees.[^cite_note-462][^cite_note-463]

### Government formation



[Oljas Bektenov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oljas_Bektenov) was reappointed prime minister for a second term in August 2026

On 23 August 2026, Tokayev announced that the government would resign following the formation of the new [Kurultai](83119061.md), as required by the new constitution, and that personnel changes would be made to give renewed impetus to its work, while stressing that these would not constitute dismissals for their own sake.[^cite_note-464][^cite_note-465][^cite_note-466] On 28 August, Tokayev signed a decree accepting the government's resignation, with Prime Minister [Oljas Bektenov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oljas_Bektenov) and other members of the cabinet continuing to perform their duties until the approval of a new composition.[^cite_note-467][^cite_note-468][^cite_note-469]

On 31 August, Tokayev nominated Bektenov for reappointment as prime minister, describing him as an experienced and qualified statesman who had performed effectively as prime minister.[^cite_note-470] The Kurultai approved his candidacy by a vote of 137–0, with seven abstentions,[^cite_note-471][^cite_note-472][^cite_note-473] following which Tokayev formally reappointed Bektenov as prime minister and formed the [Second Bektenov Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Bektenov_Government).[^cite_note-474] After his reappointment, Bektenov said the government would support Kazakhstan's development and the political changes initiated by Tokayev.[^cite_note-475] Tokayev subsequently outlined priorities for the Bektenov's government, including economic growth, effective governance, and public welfare.[^cite_note-476][^cite_note-477]
